CHAPTER 6. VISCOUS FLOWS                                                                                      144


 and, substituting this into (6.8.14), we get
                                                     √       
                                        iG0       J0 ( −iαr/a) iωt
                                uz = −         1−       √       e + c.c.                               (6.8.15)
                                        2ωρ         J0 ( −iα)

 This is Womersley flow, which was first described by Womersley in 1955. The nondimensionalised flow
 profile is                                        √       
                                ∗       i       J0 ( −iαr∗ ) it∗
                               uz = −       1−       √        e + c.c.,
                                        2        J0 ( −iα)
 where u∗z = uz /(G0 /(ωρ)), r∗ = r/a, t∗ = ωt. This nondimensionalisation shows that the flow is
 characterised by a single parameter, the Womersley number, α (as opposed to 4 dimensional parameters:
 ρ, µ, a and ω). Sketches of the flow profiles for different values of α are given in Figure~\ref{fig:womersley}
 and some movies will be shown in the lecture.




 Figure 6.32 Snapshots of sketches of Womersley flow profiles for various Womersley numbers. (a) Low
 Womersley number (profile is Poiseuille flow multiplied by a time-dependent factor), (b) intermediate
 Womersley number, (c) high Womersley number (profile is flat across the interior with a boundary layer
 in which the flow oscillates rapidly).

   Remark 6.8.11
   We may do a similar analysis for any prescribed periodic pressure gradient G(t). To do this we decom-
   pose G(t) into a Fourier series sum of terms of different frequencies. We solve for the flow generated
   by each component of G(t) individually. The total flow is the sum of these flow components.

   Remark 6.8.12
   In the human arterial system the Womersley number of the fundamental harmonic is different in
   different arteries and it takes values up to about 20.
CHAPTER 6. VISCOUS FLOWS                                                                                    145


  Remark 6.8.13
  For small Womersley numbers we use the Taylor series expansion of the Bessel function:

                                                         z2
                                          J0 (z) = 1 −      + ...
                                                         4
  to find an approximation of uz .
                                                   
                    iG0 iα2        i  αr 2                         G0 2     
               uz =           −              + . . . eiωt + c.c. ≈ −    a − r2 eiωt + c.c.
                    2ωρ    4       4 a                               8µ
                                                                     G0 2     
                                                                 =−     a − r2 cos ωt,
                                                                     4µ

  which is a Poiseuille flow profile (multiplied by an oscillating amplitude). This flow is called quasi-
  steady, because this is the solution that we would obtain if we neglected the time-derivative term in
  the Navier–Stokes equation.
CHAPTER 6. VISCOUS FLOWS                                                                                       146


  Remark 6.8.14
  For large Womersley numbers we use the standard asymptotic expansions for the Bessel function,
  which is:                                 r
                                               2        π
                                   J0 (z) ≈      cos z −
                                              πz         4
  when |z| ≫ 1. Letting z = x + iy, where x and y are real, we can expand the cosine function:
                    r
                       1  ix−iπ/4 −y                r 1                   r
                                                                                1 iz−iπ/4
                                         −ix+iπ/4 y              ix−iπ/4 −y
           J0 (z) ≈       e       e +e           e ≈           e        e =        e      .
                      2πz                                  2πz                 2πz
  where the second approximation is valid if y ≪ 0 (which is the case we have). Substituting into
  (6.8.15), we obtain
                                       r                   
                             iG0           a −√iα(1−r/a) iωt
                      uz ≈ −        1−       e                 e + c.c.
                             2ωρ           r
                                       r                                
                             iG0           a − √α (1− ar ) −i √α (1− ar ) iωt
                         =−         1−       e   2        e     2          e + c.c.
                             2ωρ           r

  Away from the boundaries, √α2 (1 − ar ) is large and so the exponential terms in the bracket in this
  expression are very small, and
                                             iG0 iωt        G0
                                    uz ≈ −       e + c.c. =    sin ωt.
                                             2ωρ            ωρ
  This type of flow is called plug flow, since at any fixed time the profile is uniform across the interior.
  Near the walls of the pipe the exponential terms become important. At the walls themselves, the
  flow is zero, and, as we move away, the expression for the velocity shows there are rapid oscillations
  that decrease in magnitude as we move away from the wall.
  Thus we see that the flow is divided into two distinct regions. There is the core in which the dominant
  balance is between the pressure gradient and the inertial term (the time-derivative term), and in
  which the viscosity plays a minor role. Sometimes this is termed an inviscid core. The other region
  is the boundary layer, in which the viscosity is important. The width of the boundary layer is of the
  order of magnitude α−1 a (often we would just say loosely that the boundary layer has width α−1 a).
  Within a few α−1 a’s away from the boundary, the flow is affected by the presence of the boundary,
  but further away the effect is negligible.
  Another way that we could find the flow for large Womersley number is to use boundary layer analysis
  in the boundary, and an inviscid flow method (such as potential flow analysis) in the interior, and
  match the two solutions at the edge of the boundary layer. Since we have the exact solution valid
  for all Womersley numbers, this method of separating into the boundary layer and core regions is
  not necessary to find the solution. However, in many problems, no solution can be found in the
  general case, but a solution can be found in the particular case when one parameter is large or small.
  It is surprising how often such solutions are possible in real world problems, and this method often
  provides significant insight into the problem.

 1. Reynolds number estimation.

 Based on Problem 2.1 in [4].
  Give an order-of-magnitude estimate of the Reynolds number in each of the following situations:
   (a) flow past the wing of a jumbo jet at 150 m/s (roughly half the speed of sound);
   (b) blood flow in a large artery (diameter 2 cm) at 0.3 m/s;
   (c) a thick layer of honey draining off a spoon at 1 cm/s;
   (d) a spermatozoon with tail length 10−3 m swimming at 100 µm/s in water.

  2. Nondimensionalisation.
 Based on Problem 2.2 in [4]
  In this problem you will work through the nondimensionalisation of a typical fluid flow problem to
  identify the Reynolds number as a key dimensionless parameter.
  Consider the two-dimensional steady flow of a viscous incompressible fluid past a circular cylinder of
  radius a. Far from the cylinder, the fluid velocity is uniform with magnitude U in the x-direction. The
  governing equations are the incompressible Navier–Stokes equations and continuity equation:
                                                1
                                   (u · ∇) u = − ∇p + ν∇2 u,           ∇ · u = 0,
                                                ρ

  where u = (u, v) is the velocity field, p is the pressure field, ρ is the fluid density, and ν is the kinematic
  viscosity of the fluid.
   (a) Write down suitable boundary conditions at the cylinder surface and far from the cylinder.
   (b) Introduce nondimensional variables
                                      x            y            u            v             p
                               x∗ =     ,   y∗ =     ,   u∗ =     ,   v∗ =     ,   p∗ =        .
                                      a            a            U            U            ρU 2
        Rewrite the governing equations and boundary conditions in terms of these nondimensional vari-
        ables.
   (c) You should have found that the nondimensional problem depends only on a single parameter: the
       Reynolds number. Thus flows at equal Reynolds numbers are geometrically similar, and one only
       needs to know the Reynolds number to understand the flow.
        This was a particularly simple problem that has an unambiguous length and velocity scale, and so
        nondimensionalisation of the problem works particularly well. However, it is frequently helpful to
        nondimensionalise problems before solving so that key dimensionless groups of parameters (such
        as the Reynolds number) can be identified.

  3. Falling ball viscometer.
  For slowly moving spheres, the resistive force is given by Stokes drag, and equals 3πµDu, where D is
  the diameter of the sphere, µ is the dynamic viscosity of the fluid, and u is the speed of the sphere (this
  is a famous result).
CHAPTER 6. VISCOUS FLOWS                                                                                 148


  (a) Check the dimensions of the Stokes drag. Is the dimension what you expect?
  (b) A steel ballbearing of mass m, diameter D falls slowly through a viscous fluid of dynamic viscosity
      µ. In this question you can neglect the buoyancy force. What are the other two forces acting on
      the ballbearing?
  (c) Write down a differential equation for the time-evolution of the velocity u of the ballbearing,
      assuming that the acceleration due to gravity is g.
  (d) Given that the ballbearing is released from rest, solve the differential equation to show that
                                                               
                                              u = ut 1 − e−t/τ ,

      where ut and τ are constant parameters that you should define. What is the physical significance
      of ut and τ ?
  (e) Sketch the velocity as a function of time.
  (f) If the ballbearing has diameter 2 mm and is falling through glycerol at 20◦ C, what is its terminal
      velocity in mm/s and what is the timescale taken to reach the terminal velocity? You may assume
      that the dynamic viscosity of glycerol at 20◦ C is 1.2 Pa s and the density of steel is 8,000 kg/m3 .
  (g) Comment on the experimental set up needed to measure the terminal velocity.

  (a) Perform a nondimensionalisation of the Navier–Stokes (6.5.1) and continuity (3.4.2) equations,
      using a scale P for the pressure and T for time, and writing the gravitational acceleration as
      g = gĝ, where ĝ is a unit vector in the direction of g.
  (b) Choose a suitable timescale T so that the inertial terms balance and multiply the Navier–Stokes
      equation by a suitable factor so that the nondimensional viscous term has coeﬀicient 1. What do
      you notice about the coeﬀicient of the inertial terms?
  (c) Hence, in the case of a low-Reynolds-number flow, Re ≪ 1, show that, to leading order, the
      equations simplify to one of the following three cases:

                                                 0 = −∇p + ρg,

      or
                                     0 = −∇p + µ∇2 u + ρg,       ∇ · u = 0,
      or
                                        0 = −∇p + µ∇2 u,      ∇ · u = 0.
      Specify the conditions under which each of these equations is obtained. In the latter two cases, we
      term the equations governing the fluid velocity the Stokes equations.
  (d) In the case of a high-Reynolds-number flow, Re ≫ 1, show that, to leading order, the equations
      simplify to one of the following three cases:

                                                 0 = −∇p + ρg,

      or
                                            Du
                                        ρ      = −∇p + ρg,    ∇ · u = 0,
                                            Dt
      or
                                           Du
                                            ρ  = −∇p, ∇ · u = 0.
                                           Dt
      Specify the conditions under which each of these equations is obtained. In the latter two cases, we
      term the equations governing the fluid velocity the Euler equations.

 1. Analysis of flow in a pipe.
 It might help to try Exercise 6.9.6 before attempting this problem.
 A viscous incompressible fluid flows steadily along an infinitely long pipe of radius a under a constant
 pressure gradient P = −dp/dz. Work in cylindrical polar coordinates (r, θ, z) with corresponding velocity
 components (ur , uθ , uz ) with the axis of the pipe aligned with r = 0.
   (a) Write down the boundary conditions to be applied.
   (b) The velocity is steady and therefore independent of t. Explain why it is also reasonable to assume
       that the velocity profile is independent of θ and z, and also that the θ-component of the velocity
       is zero.
   (c) Use the continuity equation to show that the r-component of the velocity is also zero (ur = 0).
        In cylindrical polar coordinates, the continuity equation for an incompressible fluid is
                                             1 ∂           1 ∂uθ   ∂uz
                                                  (rur ) +       +     = 0.
                                             r ∂r          r ∂θ    ∂z

   (d) Hence the only non-zero component of the velocity is the z-component, uz , and this only depends

                                                   P            

        Note that we also need to check that the velocity is smooth at r = 0. This is easier to see if we

        is u = (0, 0, uz ) with
                                                    P                   
                                              uz =      a2 − (x2 + y 2 ) ,
                                                    4µ

                                                           P

 Figure 6.38 Wavy-walled pipe with inner cylinder that emits fluid.

 Despite the fact that there is flow out from the inner cylinder in the radial direction, you may assume

                                                 du   2(aU − RR′ u)

       Note that you can actually solve this equation by instead considering the volume between z = 0

       surface r = R(z), and the two cross-sectional surfaces at z = 0 and z = z0 . The inner cylinder
       surface gives a volume flux in of 2πaU z0 , the outer cylinder surface gives no volume flux, the
       surface z = 0 gives a volume flux in of π(R(0)2 − a2 )u(0), and the surface z = z0 gives a volume
       flux out of π(R(z0 )2 − a2 )u(z0 ). Equating total volume flux in and volume flux out gives
                                2πaU z0 + π(R(0)2 − a2 )u(0) = π(R(z0 )2 − a2 )u(z0 ),
       and hence

2.3 · Exercises
2.3.2. Derivation of the material derivative.

This is the chain rule. Denote the three components of x = (x1 , x2 , x3 ). Then we differentiate through both
outer arguments of f :

                              ∂f (x(X, t), t)     ∂f    X
                                                        3
                                                           ∂xi (X, t)   ∂f
                                                =     +
                                    ∂t        X   ∂t x i=1     ∂t     X ∂xi


The factor,
                                             ∂xi    dxi
                                                  =     ≡ ui ,
                                             ∂t X   dt
corresponds to the velocity component in each direction. Then

                                      ∂f (x(X, t), t)     ∂f
                                                        =    + u · ∇f,
                                            ∂t        X   ∂t

as desired.
2.3.3. Eulerian and Lagrangian descriptions.
 (a) A particle moves with the velocity of the fluid. Then it must satisfy the equations

                              dx             dy                    d2 x    dy
                                 = −y(t),       = x(t)       ⇒          =−    = −x(t).
                              dt             dt                    dt 2    dt
     Trying solutions of the form x(t) = eλt gives λ = ±i, so the general solution is

                                               x(t) = Aeit + Be−it ,

     or, equivalently,
                                              x(t) = C cos t + D sin t,
     where C and D are constants, and
                                                     dx
                                          y(t) = −      = C sin t − D cos t.
                                                     dt
     Setting x(0) = x0 and y(0) = y0 gives C = x0 and D = −y0 , so

                                    x(t) = (x0 cos t − y0 sin t, x0 sin t + y0 cos t).
                                       p
     This is a circular path of radius x20 + y02 going anticlockwise around the origin. The velocity is

                                  u(t) = (−x0 sin t − y0 cos t, x0 cos t − y0 sin t).

 (b) The calculation works out more simply in polar coordinates; the velocity field is

                                                       u = reθ ,

       where eθ is the angular unit vector given in (B.1.6). Then the equation for particle paths is

                          p
       Thus r(t) = r0 =       x20 + y02 and θ(t) = θ0 + t, where θ0 = tan−1 (y0 /x0 ). The velocity is u(t) = r0 eθ (t).
       Note that the expression for the velocity in polar coordinates is simpler than that in Cartesian co-
       ordinates, being in one coordinate direction only. Performing this conversion makes it easier to find
       pathlines and streamlines.
     This flow is an example of rigid-body rotation, that is the fluid is moving with a velocity field that
     would be attainable for a rigid body, and there is no relative motion of the fluid particles.

3 · Equations of motion
3.6 · Exercises
3.6.1. Continuum approximation.

Salt is not like a fluid because you can make it into a pile, whereas the surface of a resting fluid is always
horizontal. However, some aspects of salt flow are similar to the flow of a fluid. A typical salt particle is
approximately a cube of side 0.5 mm, and therefore has a volume of approximately 10−10 m3 . We need a
size of hole that admits a very large number of particles. A reasonable value of the volume V appearing
in the continuum approximation might be the volume of 3 × 107 salt grains (this was the number of gas or
liquid molecules quoted in the notes), i.e. 3 × 10−3 m3 , which is a cube of side length approximately 10 cm.
We need a hole that is many times this distance, perhaps a hole with a diameter of one metre or so might
be reasonable! See also Remark 3.0.2.
3.6.2. Bump lemma.

This proof assumes that the function f is not pathological (e.g. it cannot oscillate infinitely fast, take different
values for rational vs. irrational, etc.). That’s what we mean by ”suﬀiciently smooth”.
Assume that there exists a point x0 ∈ Ω where f (x0 ) ̸= 0. Suppose without loss of generality f > 0 here.
Since f is suﬀiciently smooth (and in particular, it is continuous), then thereR exists a non-trivial neighbour-
hood near x0 where f > 0 everywhere in this neighbourhood, say V0 . Then V0 f dV = 0, contradicting the
assumption. Therefore f must be zero everywhere.
3.6.3. Leibniz’s rule.
 (a) The first circled element is associated with the quantity:
                                               Z b(t)
                                          1
                                                        [f (x, t + ∆t) − f (x, t)] dx.
                                          ∆t    a(t)

      This is essentially the finite-difference version of the first term on the RHS.
      The second circled element is associated with the quantity:
                                                                             1
                                               f (x, t)[a(t + ∆t) − a(t)]       ,
                                                                             ∆t
      and is associated with the area of the rectangle.
      The third circled element is associated with
                                                                                1
                                           f (x, t + ∆t)[b(t + ∆t) − b(t)]         ,
                                                                                ∆t
APPENDIX A. SOLUTIONS                                                                                      164

     and is associated with the area of the rectangle. Notice that for small ∆t, the rectangle height of
     f (x, t + ∆t) is approximately the same as the rectangle height of f (x, t).
 (c) Show that the Reynolds Transport Theorem in one dimension is equivalent to the Leibniz rule. Write
     the second and third terms on the RHS as
                                         Z b(t)

                                          a(t)  dx
3.6.4. Derivation of governing equations.

The proof of the continuity equation is given in Theorem 3.2.1 and the proof of the momentum equation is
given in Theorem 3.3.1, so make sure you can follow these arguments and produce your own notes.
The derivation of the Euler equations, as given in Definition 3.4.5 requires the additional assumption of an
incompressible fluid, following Theorem 3.4.1. Incompressibility implies that ∇ · u = 0, and this equation
then replaces the continuity equation.
3.6.5. Pressure field in uniform flow.
 (a) The fluid has a uniform velocity, which means that all components of ∇u vanish. The Euler equation
     simplifies to
                                                 ∂u
                                               ρ    = −∇p − ρgk,
                                                 ∂t
     where
                                                ∂u
                                                    = (b, 3t2 , et ).
                                                 ∂t
     Hence, the pressure is given by solving the equation,

                                              ∂u
                                    ∇p = −ρ      − ρgk = −ρ( b, 3t2 , et + g ),
                                              ∂t
     which is a vector equation. Splitting into components, we obtain

                                  ∂p            ∂p              ∂p
                                     = −ρb,        = −3ρt2 ,       = −ρ(et + g).
                                  ∂x            ∂y              ∂z
     Solving sequentially, the first equation gives

                                              p = −ρbx + f1 (y, z, t).

     Substituting into the second equation,

                                    ∂f1
                                        = −3ρt2 ⇒ f1 = −3ρt2 y + f2 (z, t),
                                    ∂y

                                           p = −ρbx − 3ρt2 y + f2 (z, t).
     The third equation then implies
                                 ∂f2
                                     = −ρ(et + g) ⇒ f2 = −ρ(et + g)z + f3 (t),
                                 ∂z
                                      p = −ρbx − 3ρt2 y − ρ(et + g)z + f3 (t).
     Using p = p0 at the origin gives f3 (t) = p0 for all t. Hence
                                                                         
                                        p = p0 − ρ bx + 3t2 y + (et + g)z .

 (b) The pressure is constant on parallel planes normal to the vector (b, 3t2 , et + g), as illustrated below:
APPENDIX A. SOLUTIONS                                                                                       165




     Figure A.1 Contours of the pressure field at fixed t.
3.6.6. Piston problem.

We assume that the fluid pressure at the open end of the pipe is atmospheric pressure. Since the fluid moves
as a rigid body, the velocity field has no spatial gradients, and hence the Euler equation reduces to

                                                      ∂u
                                                  ρ      = −∇p,
                                                      ∂t

where the velocity is uniformly u = (Ẋ(t), 0).
Solving for p and using the fact that p is atmospheric pressure at x = L, we have

                                                      d2 X
                                        p(x, t) = ρ        (L − x) + patm .
                                                      dt2

At the piston x = X, note that atmospheric pressure applies in addition to F , meaning that the force on the
fluid is
                                               F + Apatm ,
and hence the fluid pressure is
                                                      F
                                                        + patm .
                                                      A
Then
                       F           d2 X                                d2 X       F
                         + patm = ρ 2 (L − X) + patm               ⇒        =           .
                       A           dt                                  dt2    Aρ(L − X)

Note that the atmospheric pressure cancels in this problem, and this is common to problems of this type,
because atmospheric pressure acts uniformly on all surfaces exposed to the atmosphere and therefore does
not lead to a resultant force or affect the dynamics. This motivates the introduction of a gauge pressure,
pgauge , whereby a uniform and constant baseline pressure is chosen, in this case patm , with the gauge pressure
given by pgauge = p − patm . Since the pressure only appears as a gradient in the governing equations, using
the gauge pressure does not affect the result. Introducing a gauge pressure can make the algebra simpler.
APPENDIX A. SOLUTIONS                                                                                  166

3.6.8. Using streamlines: The clepsydra.
 (a) The fluid velocity at the surface of the jar satisfies u · n = 0, so that no fluid leaves or enters the jar
     through its sides. This means that the flow next to the boundary of the jar must be parallel to this
     boundary, meaning that streamlines are parallel to the boundary. Equivalently, the boundary r = a(z)
     is a streamline.
 (b) We apply Bernoulli’s equation to equate the energy at a point at the edge of the free surface at z = h(t)
     and the fluid leaving
                     √     the hole at z = 0. At the free surface, the pressure is atmospheric, p = patm , and
     the speed is uS 1 + a′ 2 . At the hole, the pressure is also atmospheric, p = patm , and the velocity is u.
     Thus, Bernoulli’s equation for the streamline joining the two points gives
                                     patm  1          ′           patm  1
                                          + u2S (1 + a 2 ) + gh =      + u2 .
                                       ρ   2                        ρ   2

     Assuming that uS ≪ u, this simplifies to
                                                            1 2
                                                     gh =     u .
                                                            2

 (c) The volume flux out of the hole is πa2h u, while the rate of decrease of the volume of fluid in the jar is
     πa(h(t))2 uS . Conservation of mass therefore gives

                                                  πa2 uS = πa2h u,

     or
                                                            a2h
                                                     uS =       u.
                                                            a2
     Using the result from part (b) to eliminate u gives

                                                         a2h p
                                                  uS =        2gh.
                                                         a2
     We require uS to be independent of time so that equally spaced intervals are traversed in equal times.
     Hence,
                                                2 √ 1/2
                                                 ah 2g
                                           a=                h1/4 .
                                                   uS
     The terms in the brackets are constant, so we have that the radius of the jar must vary as the fourth
     root of the height.

  4.7.2. Evaluation of the flux.

  This is a nice question to do because there is a simple recipe. For each of the complex potentials in the
  previous question, find the imaginary part to obtain the streamfunction. Then evaluate the difference in
  streamfunction values at the start and end values of the desired curve.
  For uniform flow, ψ(r, θ) = U r sin(θ − α)|. For the case of a circle, the flux is:
                                             |ψ(1, 2π) − ψ(1, 0)| = 0.

                                            |ψ(1, 0) − ψ(1, π/2)| = 0.

  4.7.9. Elementary conformal maps.

  A conformal map is a mapping ζ = g(z) where g is analytic and its derivative is nonzero in some region
  of the complex plane.
  The map from the wedge to the upper half-plane is given in the notes near (4.5.1). It is ζ = z π/α . The
  points where the mapping is not conformal are the critical points listed in the figure Figure 4.13. Point A
  at infinity is mapped directly to +infinity. The origin is mapped to the origin. And the point, B, found
  via z = reiα , is sent to ζ = −rπ/α and then afterwards r → ∞.

5 · Water waves
5.5 · Exercises
5.5.1. Derivation of the kinematic condition.

By the definition of the material derivative, we have that

                     D(y − η)   ∂(y − η)                         ∂η
                              =          + (u, v) · ∇(y − η) = −    + (u, v) · (−ηx , 1).
                       Dt          ∂t                            ∂t
Equating the above to zero for the case of y = η(x, t) yields the desired result.
APPENDIX A. SOLUTIONS                                                                                    185

5.5.2. Stokes waves in finite depth.

Note that the finite-depth linear wave theory was derived as part of the problem class, so you can examine
the video there for more details. Firstly, we look to linearise the two surface boundary conditions,
                                            ∂ϕ    ∂η   ∂ϕ ∂η
                                                =    +       ,
                                             ∂y   ∂t   ∂x ∂x
                                           ∂ϕ 1
                                              + |∇ϕ|2 + gη = 0,
                                           ∂t   2
which are imposed on y = η(x). The quadratic terms can be ignored by assuming that the fluid is linearised
about a small disturbance. Hence η is assumed to be small. Similarly, the velocity terms are expanded about
y = 0. For example,
                                   ∂ϕ        ∂ϕ        ∂2ϕ
                                           =         +          η + ....
                                   ∂x y=η    ∂x y=0 ∂x2 y=0
Thus we can approximate this quantity to leading order by
                                             ∂ϕ       ∂ϕ
                                                    ∼        .
                                             ∂x y=η   ∂x y=0

Therefore, both kinematic and dynamic conditions are simplified in this manner, yielding
                                                 ∂ϕ    ∂η
                                                    =     ,
                                                 ∂y    ∂t
                                                ∂ϕ
                                                   + gη = 0,
                                                ∂t
now imposed on y = 0.
We substitute now the ansatzes

                                η = A cos(kx − ωt),      ϕ = f (y) sin(kx − ωt),

into the governing equations.
Written in terms of f , we must now solve the following system:

                                                f ′′ + k 2 f = 0,
                                                 f ′ (−h) = 0,
                                                f ′ (0) = (ω)A,
                                            −(ω)f (0) + gA = 0.

Now instead of writing the solution for f in terms of the two exponentials e±ky , it is better to write the
general solution of the second-order ODE as

                                          f (y) = B cosh[k(y − y0 )],

where y0 is constant. We can then see that if y0 = −h, then the boundary condition f ′ (−h) = 0 is satisfied.
The last two boundary conditions, evaluated on y = 0 yield
                                                        
                                       ω   −k sinh(kh)     A
                                                             = 0.                                    (A.0.1)
                                       g −(ω) cosh(kh)     B

For there to be non-trivial solutions, we thus need

                                             (ω)2 = gk tanh(kh),
APPENDIX A. SOLUTIONS                                                                                       186

and therefore there are two possible wave speeds, namely
                                                 g               1/2
                                           c=∓         tanh(kh)          ,
                                                   k
and both are simply negatives of one another; so represent left and rightwards propagating waves of the same
speed.
5.5.3. Linear waves in a current.
 (a) We begin with the governing equations as shown in Theorem 5.1.2.
     On the channel bottom, we expect that the vertical velocity is zero, hence
                                               ∂ϕ
                                                  = 0,        y = −h,
                                               ∂y
     while at upstream infinity, we expect the the velocity to be uniform and of speed U . Hence in terms of
     the potential,
                                             ϕ ∼ U x,      x → −∞.

     Indeed, this suggests that we should linearise about the uniform flow, with
                                                    ϕ = U x + ϕ̂,
     where ϕ̂ is the approximation, which we expect to be small (as is standard in linear problems, we often
     ignore the hat notation).
 (b) The linearisation process for the kinematic condition remains largely the same, except we would pro-
     duce:                                      "         #
                                           ∂η         ∂ ϕ̂ ∂η    ∂ ϕ̂
                                              + U+            =       .
                                           ∂t         ∂x ∂x      ∂y
     Again quadratic terms are ignored, and we see that the requisite condition becomes (dropping the hat):
                                         ∂η    ∂η   ∂ϕ
                                            +U    =    ,           y = η(x, t).
                                         ∂t    ∂x   ∂y

     The linearisation of Bernoulli’s equation is done similarly.
     When considering the Bernoulli equation,
                                          ∂ϕ 1        p
                                             + |∇ϕ|2 + + gy = F (t),
                                          ∂t  2       ρ
     it is prudent to take the choice of F (t) so that the inertial term is removed. Specifically, if we take
                                                          U2   patm
                                                F (t) =      +      ,
                                                          2     ρ
     this would remove the leading term from inertia.
     The only difference with the usual linearisation process is the inertial term:
                                                      !2        !2
                                                 ∂ ϕ̂      ∂ ϕ̂           ∂ ϕ̂
                                |∇ϕ| = U +
                                    2
                                                         +         = 2U        + ...
                                                 ∂x        ∂y             ∂x

     so now Bernoulli’s equation yields (dropping hats):
                                         ∂ϕ    ∂ϕ
                                            +U    + gη = 0,                  y = 0,
                                         ∂t    ∂x
     where we have placed the boundary condition on y = 0 as standard.
APPENDIX A. SOLUTIONS                                                                                      187

 (c) At this point, the linear solution follows similar steps to the case of finite depth with zero current. We
     have η = A cos(kx − ωt) and ϕ = f (y) sin(kx − ωt).
     Written in terms of f , we must now solve the following system:

                                                        f ′′ + k 2 f = 0,
                                                         f ′ (−h) = 0,
                                                     f ′ (0) = (ω − U k)A,
                                               −(ω − U k)f (0) + gA = 0,

     The first two equations yield the typical solution for the case of finite depth:

                                                   f (y) = B cosh[k(y + h)].

     The last two conditions yield
                                                                            
                                          ω − Uk       −k sinh(kh)            A
                                                                                 = 0.                  (A.0.2)
                                             g      −(ω − U k) cosh(kh)       B

     For there to be non-trivial solutions, we thus need

                                               (ω − U k)2 = gk tanh(kh),

     and therefore there are two possible wave speeds, namely
                                                           g               1/2
                                               c± = U ∓          tanh(kh)          .
                                                             k
                                     p
     In the deep water limit, if U < g/k this yields subcritical solutions, and it is possible to have a wave
     pattern that is non-trivial upstream of the flow.
5.5.4. Fluids of different densities.

The kinematic condition(s) must be exactly the same form as the usual kinematic condition, since the density
of the fluids play no role. They arise from setting D(y−η)
                                                      Dt   = 0, for both fluids. This gives,
                                      ∂ϕi ∂η   ∂ϕi    ∂η   ∂ϕi
                               ηt +          =     =⇒    =                     on y = 0,
                                      ∂x ∂x    ∂y     ∂t   ∂y
after linearising, and as imposed on i = 1, 2.
The dynamic condition expresses the fact that the pressures are equal at the interface between the two fluids.
Therefore at the interface, we have (ignoring quadratic terms), from Theorem 5.1.1
                                               ∂ϕ1   p1
                                                   +    + gη = B1 ,
                                                ∂t   ρ1
                                               ∂ϕ2   p2
                                                   +    + gη = B2 .
                                                ∂t   ρ2
However at the interface, we need the pressures to be equal. Therefore we can set p1 = p2 . Subtracting the
two equations then yields
                                                                            
                             ∂ϕ1            p     p                     ∂ϕ2
                        ρ1       + gη +        −    − B1 + B2 = ρ2          + gη
                              ∂t            ρ1   ρ2                      ∂t
as applied on y = 0. We are free to pick the constants, so we pick them so as to completely zero the expression
in the second group of brackets. Thus
                                                                     
                                           ∂ϕ1                ∂ϕ2
                                      ρ1        + gη = ρ2          + gη
                                            ∂t                 ∂t
APPENDIX A. SOLUTIONS                                                                                       188

on y = 0.
So now the natural question is to ask how the linearisation procedure changes with the two fluids of different
densities. Gain, we set η = A cos(kx − ωt) and ϕi = fi (y) sin(kx − ωt). The equation for f is similarly given
by f ′′ = k 2 f, and this time, we must select different exponential powers so as to ensure the fluid is bounded
either as y → ∞ or as y → −∞. This essentially yields

                                                 f1 = Beky ,       y < 0,
                                                        −ky
                                               f2 = Ce        ,    y > 0,

for constants B and C.
The remaining boundary conditions are:

                                                    f1′ (0) = (ω)A,
                                                    f2′ (0) = (ω)A,
                                   ρ1 (−ωf1 (0) + gA) = ρ2 (−ωf2 (0) + gA),

thus

                                                      Bk = ωA,
                                                     −Ck = ωA,
                                         ρ1 (−ωB + gA) = ρ2 (−ωC + gA),

which in matrix form is                                             
                                      ω             −k         0      A     0
                                     ω              0         k       
                                                                     B = 0 .
                                  g(ρ1 − ρ2 )      −ωρ1       ωρ2     C     0

Expanding the determinant about the first row yields

                                           0       k         ω               k
                                     ω                +k
                                          −ωρ1    ωρ2    g(ρ1 − ρ2 )        ρ2 ω
                                         = ω 2 k(ρ1 + ρ2 ) − k 2 g(ρ1 − ρ2 ) = 0,

which gives the desired result.
5.5.5. Fluids with surface tension.

This question was done in the problem class recording on 11 November 2025, accessed here.
Very little changes in the linearisation procedure with surface tension, as the only difference is the Bernoulli
equation, which is linearised to give
                                               ∂ϕ         T ∂2η
                                                  + gη =         ,
                                               ∂t         ρ ∂x2
evaluated on y = 0.
Thus in this equation, we use the same ansatzes as for the previous few questions. The system of equations
is

                                                    f ′′ + k 2 f = 0,
                                                 f (−∞) is bounded,
                                                    f ′ (0) = (ω)A,
                                         −(ω)f (0) + [g + (T /ρ)k 2 ]A = 0,
APPENDIX A. SOLUTIONS                                                                                         189

The derivation must be exactly the same as for infinite-depth gravity waves, and the only difference is
that instead of g, we can substitute g + (T /ρ)k 2 when we impose the boundary conditions and apply the
determinant condition.
Previously for deep-water gravity waves, we had ω 2 = gk. So now we must have
                                                          
                                             2         T 2
                                           ω = g + k k,
                                                       ρ

as desired.
5.5.6. The dispersion relation (simple).
 (a)                                                                                            
                                       (k1 + k2 )x − (ω1 + ω2 )t         (k1 − k2 )x − (ω1 − ω2 )t
                       η(x, t) = 2 sin                             cos
                                                   2                                 2

 (b) Substituting these expressions, we have

                                           η = 2 sin(kx − ωt) cos(∆kx − ∆ωt).

       The point here is that ∆k, ∆ω ≪ 1, so the cosine term is very nearly 1. This yields the standard
       travelling sinusoidal wave. However, over long timescales, notably when t is large, the cosine term is
       no longer constant. This yields a slowly varying envelope or amplitude multiplying the sine,
 (c) This part is quite straightforward. The maxima of the cosines are found at multiples of 2π in the
     argument. For example, the first maxima lies at ∆kx − ∆ωt = 0, hence x(t) = (∆k/∆ω)t.
       This is indeed equivalent to a particle that moves with velocity ∆k/∆ω. In the limit the perturbations
       to the quantities tends to zero, we can informally note that this leads to the notion of the group velocity,
                                                               dω
                                                        vg =      .
                                                               dk

6.9 · Practice Exercises
6.9.1. Reynolds number estimation.
 (a) The kinematic viscosity of air at standard temperature and pressure is about 1.5 × 10−5 m2 /s. The
     wingspan of a jumbo jet is about 60 m, so the Reynolds number is approximately
                                                  UL    150 × 60
                                           Re =      =            = 6 × 108 .
                                                   ν   1.5 × 10−5

 (b) The kinematic viscosity of blood is about 2.4 × 10−6 m2 /s. The diameter of the artery is about 0.02 m,
     so the Reynolds number is approximately
                                                UL      0.3 × 0.02
                                            Re =    =               = 2500.
                                                 ν     2.4 × 10−6
       Although this is above 2000, blood flow in arteries is typically laminar.
 (c) The kinematic viscosity of honey is about 0.007 m2 /s. The thickness of the layer is about 0.005 m, so
     the Reynolds number is approximately
                                                  UL   0.01 × 0.005
                                           Re =      =              ≈ 0.007.
                                                   ν      0.007

 (d) The kinematic viscosity of water at room temperature is about 10−6 m2 /s. The length of the tail is
     about 10−3 m, so the Reynolds number is approximately
                                                 UL   100 × 10−6 × 10−3
                                          Re =      =                   = 0.1.
                                                  ν          10−6
APPENDIX A. SOLUTIONS                                                                                  190

       The physical significance of these choices is that ut is the terminal velocity of the sphere and τ is the

 (g) The ball would attain its terminal velocity very quickly (in much less than a second), and since ut is on
     order of centimetres per second, a container of about 10 cm deep would be suitable for measurement. In
     fact this idea is commonly used to measure the viscosity of fluids (since the viscosity can be calculated
     from the terminal velocity); this is called a falling ball viscometer.
6.9.4. Stress on a plate.
 (a) We apply no-slip boundary conditions at the plate. Thus if y = kx then we must have u = 0 and v = 0.
     Hence
                                 0 = αkx + Ax + B, 0 = −Akx + Dx + C,

                                         u = α (y − kx) ,   v = αk (y − kx) .

6.9.5. Stress on a surface due to the pressure.
 (a) The force on x = x0 is pressure times area acting into the cube, which is δ 2 p|x0 i, while the force on
     x = x0 + δ equals −δ 2 p|x+δ i. Hence the contribution to the force from these two faces is

                                                                        ∂p        ∂p
                                      δ 2 p|x0 i − δ 2 p|x+δ i ≈ −δ 3      i = −V    i.
                                                                        ∂x        ∂x

(b) The force on y = y0 is δ 2 p|y0 j and that on y = y0 + δ is −δ 2 p|y+δ j. This gives a contribution

                                                                        ∂p        ∂p
                                      δ 2 p|y0 j − δ 2 p|y+δ j ≈ −δ 3      j = −V    j.
                                                                        ∂y        ∂y

     Similarly, the force on z = z0 is δ 2 p|z0 k and that on z = z0 + δ is −δ 2 p|z+δ k. This gives a contribution

                                                                        ∂p        ∂p
                                     δ 2 p|z0 k − δ 2 p|z+δ k ≈ −δ 3       k = −V    k.
                                                                        ∂z        ∂z
     Hence the total force on the fluid in the cube due to the pressure of the surrounding fluid is given by

                                              ∂p     ∂p     ∂p
                                         −V      i−V    j−V    k = −V ∇p.
                                              ∂x     ∂y     ∂z

 (c) The unit normal to the face x = x0 is n̂ = −i, and hence the stress at a point in this face is

                                               τ = σ n̂ = −pI(−i) = pi.

     Hence the force on this face equals δ 2 px=x0 i. Similarly, the unit normal to the face x = x0 + δ is n̂ = i,
     and hence the stress at a point in this face is

                                               τ = σ n̂ = −pI(i) = −pi.
APPENDIX A. SOLUTIONS                                                                                    194

     Hence the force on this face equals −δ 2 px=x0 +δ i. Thus the contribution to the force from these two
     faces is
                                                                  ∂p      ∂p
                                   δ 2 p|x0 i − δ 2 p|x+δ i ≈ −δ 3 i = −V    i.
                                                                  ∂x      ∂x
     The contributions from the other faces can be calculated similarly, and the total force on the fluid in
     the cube due to the pressure of the surrounding fluid is given by

                                           ∂p     ∂p     ∂p

6.9.6. Analysis of flow in a channel.
 (a) The no-slip boundary conditions give

                                         u(x, −h, z) = 0,   u(x, h, z) = 0

     for all x and z.
(b) Since the plates are infinite in the x and z directions, and the pressure gradient is constant, there
    is no preferred location in these directions, and hence the velocity profile is independent of x and z.
    Furthermore, since there is no pressure gradient in the z direction, there is no driving force for flow in
    this direction, and hence the z-component of the velocity is zero (w = 0).
 (c) The continuity equation for an incompressible fluid is

                                                ∂u ∂v   ∂w
                                                  +   +    = 0.
                                                ∂x ∂y   ∂z
     Since the velocity is independent of x and z, the first and third terms are zero, and hence
                                                     ∂v
                                                        = 0.
                                                     ∂y
     Since the plates are rigid boundaries, there is no flow through them, and hence the no-penetration
     boundary conditions give
                                        v(x, −h, z) = 0, v(x, h, z) = 0
     for all x and z. Since v is independent of y and zero at both y = −h and y = h, it follows that v = 0
     for all y.
(d) At y = −h, we have
                                             P              P 2
                                 u(−h) =        h2 − (−h)2 =    (h − h2 ) = 0,
                                             2µ              2µ
     and at y = h, we have
                                             P           P 2
                                    u(h) =      h2 − h2 =    (h − h2 ) = 0.
                                             2µ           2µ
     Hence the profile satisfies the boundary conditions.
 (e) A larger pressure gradient P means a larger driving force for the flow, and hence a larger velocity. A
     larger viscosity µ means a larger resistance to the flow, and hence a smaller velocity. Therefore, it is
     to be expected that the velocity profile increases as P increases and decreases as µ increases.
 (f) The pressure at a general point is given by

                                                   p = p0 − P x.

     At y = −h, the normal stress is given by the pressure, pointing into the plate:

                                               τy = −p = −p0 + P x.
APPENDIX A. SOLUTIONS                                                                                         195

     (note the negative sign as ”into the plate” is in the −y-direction). The x-component of the shear stress
     at y = −h is given by
                                                                         
                                      ∂u       ∂ P 2                      P
                               τx = µ     =µ           (h − y 2 ) = µ − y = P h
                                      ∂y      ∂y 2µ                       µ

     (note that this is positive since the fluid is pushing the plate in the positive x-direction). The z-
     component of the shear stress at y = −h is zero since w = 0. Hence

                                               τ = (P h, −p0 + P x, 0).

     At y = h, the normal stress is given by the pressure, pointing into the plate:

                                                    τ y = p0 − P x

     (note the sign change compared to the previous part as ”into the plate” is in the +y-direction). The
     x-component of the shear stress at y = h is given by
                                                                        
                                   ∂u        ∂ P 2                     P
                           τx = −µ     = −µ          (h − y 2 ) = −µ − y = P h.
                                   ∂y       ∂y 2µ                       µ

     Note that the formula used has a negative sign as ”into the fluid” is in the −y-direction. The overall
     sign is positive since the fluid is pushing the plate in the positive x-direction. (It is good to check signs
     after this kind of calculation by checking the direction of the force.) The z-component of the shear
     stress at y = h is zero since w = 0. Hence

                                                τ = (P h, p0 − P x, 0).

 (g) The normal stresses on the two plates are equal in magnitude but opposite in direction, as expected
     since the pressure acts equally on both plates, but ”into the plates” is a different direction.
     The shear stresses on the two plates are equal in magnitude and direction, indicating that the fluid is
     exerting the same tangential force on both plates in the direction of the flow. This is also expected
     since the plane y = 0 is a plane of symmetry.

    Combining these three results gives
                                                 ZZZ
                                                                                
                                        F =                 −∇p + 2µ∇2 u − µ∇2 u dV
                                                 Z Z ZV
                                                                       
                                            =               −∇p + µ∇2 u dV,
                                                       V

    as required.
(c) If the blob is small, then the integrand in the expression for the force varies little over the volume of
    the blob, and so                                            
                                            F ≈ −∇p + µ∇2 u V,
    where V is the volume of the blob. Hence the force per unit volume is given by
                                                           F
                                                             = −∇p + µ∇2 u,
                                                           V
    as required.

6.9.10. Derivation of the Cauchy equation.
 (a) We consider a volume V with surface S consisting of a small cube of side length δ, as shown in
     Figure 6.18. We apply the Reynolds Transport Theorem to the fluid in the cube:
                            ZZZ                    ZZZ                     
                          d                                   ∂f
                                      f dx dy dz =               + ∇ · (f u) dx dy dz,
                         dt     V (t)                  V (t) ∂t

     where f is any function, a vector or a scalar.
     Setting f = ρu equal to the momentum per unit volume in the fluid, the integral equals the momentum
     of the fluid in the cube, and the time derivative is the rate of change of momentum, which is the total
     force acting on the fluid in the cube.
     The total force equals the sum of the surface forces and body forces. The body force equals the force
     of gravity: ρV g. The surface forces are an integral:
                                             ZZ           ZZ

 (a) We have

     The coeﬀicient multiplying the inertial terms is the Reynolds number Re.
 (c) In this case, the inertial terms are small compared to the viscous terms, so we can neglect them. The
     gravitational acceleration term has order of magnitude ρL2 g/(µU ). We need to consider the cases in
     which this is large, order one or small:
       • If ρL2 g/(µU ) ≫ 1, the pressre have a predominantly hydrostatic pressure profile, with the fluid dy-
         namical problem being a small perturbation to the background static problem. Thus the pressure

         gradient balances this term:

         fluid flow. Thus the dimensional problem is

                                                   0 = −∇p + ρg.

6.10 · Problems set 7 to hand in
6.10.1. Analysis of flow in a pipe.
 (a) The no-slip boundary conditions give
                                                       u|r=a = 0.

(b) Assuming axisymmetric flow implies there is no dependence on θ and that the θ-component of the
    velocity is zero uθ = 0. Since the pipe is infinitely long in the z-direction, and the pressure gradient is
    constant, there is no preferred location in this directions, and hence the velocity profile is independent
    of z.
 (c) We have no θ- or z-dependence, meaning that the continuity equation reduces to

                                                   1 ∂
                                                        (rur ) = 0,
                                                   r ∂r
     and hence ur = A/r, where A is constant. Since the cylinder is a solid boundary, we need ur = 0 at
     r = a. Therefore, A = 0, and so ur = 0 everywhere.
(d) At r = a, we have
                                                       P         
                                                uz =      a2 − a2 = 0,
                                                       4µ
     which satisfies the no-slip boundary condition.
APPENDIX A. SOLUTIONS                                                                                     206

 (e) A larger pressure gradient P means a larger driving force for the flow, and hence a larger velocity. A
     larger viscosity µ means a larger resistance to the flow, and hence a smaller velocity. Therefore, it is
     to be expected that the velocity profile increases as P increases and decreases as µ increases.
 (f) The pressure at a general point is given by

                                                   p = p0 + P z.

     The stress vector is given by τ = σn, with n = −r̂ and σ = −pI + 2µe. Simplifying (6.4.7), we have
                                                                         
                                         0   0 12 ∂u z       0     0 − P4µr
                                                   ∂r
                                                                           
                                 e= 0       0    0 = 0          0  0 .
                                       1 ∂uz
                                       2 ∂r  0    0         − P4µr 0  0

     Hence,                                                                   
                                      −p0 − P z           0           −P r/2
                                   σ=    0           −p0 − P z          0     .
                                       −P r/2             0          −p0 − P z
     At r = a, we have

                                              τ =σn
                                                = (−pI + 2µe) (−r̂)
                                                = (pI − 2µe) r̂
                                                         ∂uz
                                                =pr̂ − µ     ẑ.
                                                         ∂r
     Hence the normal component of the stress vector is

                                                τr = p = p0 + P z,

     and the axial component of the shear stress vector is
                                                         ∂uz
                                                 τz = −µ
                                                         ∂r     
                                                  ∂ P 2
                                             = −µ       (a − r )
                                                               2
                                                  ∂r 4µ
                                                            
                                                         P
                                                = −µ − r
                                                         2µ
                                                      Pa
                                                    =
                                                       2
     The θ-component of the stress vector is zero:

                                                      τθ = 0.

 (g) The normal stress is due to the pressure in the fluid, which acts equally in all directions, and hence
     the normal stress on the inside of the pipe wall points radially outward into the wall. The shear stress
     points along the pipe in the direction of increasing z since this is the direction of the flow, and hence
     the fluid exerts a tangential force on the pipe wall in this direction.

APPENDIX A. SOLUTIONS                                                                                     207

                                         i j             k

                                         x y             z
     Hence the velocity components are

                                               ∂u ∂v   ∂w

                                                      ∂y
                                                        
                                                ∂v    ∂w
                                       σ23 =µ      +       = µ(0 + 0) = 0,
                                                ∂z    ∂y
                                                        
                                                ∂w ∂u
                                       σ31 =µ       +      = µ(0 + 0) = 0,
                                                ∂x    ∂z
                                                        
                                                ∂w ∂v
                                       σ32 =µ       +      = µ(0 + 0) = 0,
                                                ∂y    ∂z
                                                      ∂w
                                       σ33 = − p + 2µ     = −p + 0 = −p.
                                                      ∂z
     Hence the deviatoric stress tensor is
                                                               
                                             −p          0    0
                                          σ= 0         −p    0  , = −pI,
                                              0          0   −p

     where I is the identity tensor.
 (d) The result shows that the deviatoric stress tensor is zero, meaning there are no viscous stresses in this
     flow. The only stress is due to the pressure in the fluid. This is as expected since the flow represents
     a solid body rotation with angular velocity Ω. In a solid body rotation, there is no relative motion
     between adjacent fluid particles, and hence no viscous stresses.
6.10.3. Flow outside a rotating cylinder.
 (a) The flow and pressure are steady since the cylinder rotates at constant angular velocity. The flow
     and pressure have rotational symmetry and therefore no dependence on θ. The flow and pressure are
     independent of z since the cylinder is infinite in this direction and there is no pressure gradient in
     this direction. The z-component of velocity is zero since there is no motion of the cylinder or pressure
     gradient in this direction.
APPENDIX A. SOLUTIONS                                                                                      208

(b) Since there is no θ- or z-dependence, the continuity equation reduces to
                                                  1 ∂
                                                       (rur ) = 0,
                                                  r ∂r
    and hence ur = A/r, where A is constant. Since the cylinder is a solid boundary, we need ur = 0 at
    r = a. Therefore, A = 0, and so ur = 0 everywhere.
(c) Since ur = uz = 0 and there is no θ- or z-dependence, the Navier–Stokes equations reduce to

    The z-component shows that p = p(r). Now consider the θ-component. Substituting uθ = a2 Ω/r:

    which matches the velocity of the cylinder surface.
(d) Initially, there will be a thin boundary layer of fluid close to the cylinder surface that starts to rotate
    with the cylinder due to the no-slip boundary condition. As time progresses, viscous diffusion will
    cause the rotation to spread outwards from the cylinder surface into the fluid. Eventually, the flow will
    reach a steady state, which is the flow found in the previous part.

       where U is the characteristic velocity, a is the characteristic length scale (the radius of the sphere), ρ
       is the density of the fluid, and µ is the shear viscosity of the fluid.
 (b)
          • In case (a), the flow is smooth and steady around the sphere, and the flow is also reversible
            (left–right symmetry), indicating a low Reynolds number.
          • In case (b), there is some disturbance in the flow behind the sphere, suggesting a moderate
            Reynolds number, of order 100.
       • In case (c), there is a turbulent wake behind the sphere, indicating a high Reynolds number,
         Re ≫ 103 .
6.11.2. Channel flow.
 (a) We have u = v = w = 0 at y = ±d.
 (b) Fully-developed flow implies the velocity components do not depend on x. Thus, the continuity equation
     reduces to
                                                    ∂v
                                                        = 0,
                                                    ∂y
       implying that v is independent of y. The no-slip boundary conditions at each plate implies v = 0 there.
       Hence, v = 0 everywhere.
 (c) The three components of the Navier–Stokes equations simplify to

 (e) For the plate at y = k, the x-component of the stress is

                                                              ∂u
                                                     τ = −µ
                                                              ∂y

       (the minus sign occurs since the vector into the fluid points in the −y-direction) and the z-component
       of the stress is zero since the flow is two-dimensional. The x-component equals
                                                               
                                                            Gd

                                                             µ

       For the plate at y = −k, the x-component of the stress is
                                                             ∂u
                                                      τ =µ
                                                             ∂y

                                                                

                                                             µ

       Thus the stress at both plates is Gd in the +x-direction, and the force on the whole plate is GdA in
       the +x-direction. Hence the tethering force to keep each plate fixed is GdA in the −x-direction.
     Note that it is very easy to make a sign error on this type of question. I would therefore recommend
     always reflecting on your answer to see if the direction you get is what you’d expect from the physics.
     A viscous fluid tries to drag the surface along with the flow, and so the force due to the shear stress is
     sometimes called the drag force. Thus, the tethering force must oppose this drag force, which is what
     we found.

 (a)

    Figure A.9 Wavy-walled pipe – planes to apply mass conservation.
    The volumetric flux into the left-hand surface of this region equals area times velocity:
                                                   π(R2 − a2 )u
    and that out of the right-hand surface equals
                                                                  
                      π (R + dR)2 − a2 (u + du) = π (R + R′ dz) − a2 (u + u′ dz) ,
                                                               2


    where we used the chain rule to rewrite the increments in terms of dz. Note that throughout this
    equation, we neglect dz 2 and higher powers, as they are small.
(b) The area of the inner cylinder in this region is 2πadz, and thus we have a volumetric flux 2πaU dz
    entering this region from the inner cylinder.
(c) Since the volume of the pipe between z and z + dz is fixed, the total volumetric flux into this region
    equals the total volumetric flux out.
    Thus
                                                                   
                         π(R2 − a2 )u + 2πaU dz = π (R + R′ dz) − a2 (u + u′ dz) ,

                                                          
                       ⇒ 2πaU dz = π (R2 − a2 )u′ + 2RR′ u dz,
                       ⇒ 2aU = (R2 − a2 )u′ + 2RR′ u,
                                 2(aU − RR′ u)
                       ⇒ u′ =                  ,
                                    R 2 − a2
    thus proving the result.
(d) A suitable lengthscale is R0 . Simplifying the differential equation for u, by neglecting variations in
    pipe radius R′ and the radius of the inner cylinder a, gives
                                                   du  2aU

                                                   dz   R0
    suggesting a suitable velocity scale is this times L (the velocity that would be attained at the end of

                                                      2aLU

    Hence the Reynolds number is
                                                 ρR0 2aLU      2aLU ρ

    Many students did not find an expression for the velocity scale, and just left it as u(z) or similar.
    This would mean that we do not have an expression for the Reynolds number in terms of the problem
    parameters. Also, many students did not approximate R − a as R0 , which simplifies the expression
    (some did get R0 − a, but you can go further than this).
    I have added a final sentence to the question to clarify that the answer should be in terms of the
    problem parameters as very few students attempted to find an order of magnitude for u(z).
APPENDIX A. SOLUTIONS                                                                                 212

(e) The Reynolds number would need to be large (but not so large that the flow becomes turbulent).
    In the limit of large Reynolds number, we would recover the Euler equation from the Navier–Stokes
    equation (see Exercise 6.9.11). In this limit we only need a no-penetration condition (u · n̂ = 0) and
    not the full no-slip conditions (u = 0), and so in this case uniform flow is possible.
 (f) The flow would need to satisfy the no-slip boundary condition at the outer cylinder and the velocity
     there would therefore tend to zero.

B.1 Converting between different coordinate systems
It is often convenient to convert a problem from one coordinate system to another, and, although converting
scalar quantities is reasonably straightforward, converting vector quantities (such as the fluid velocity vector)
is more complicated. This section briefly goes through how to convert vectors and scalars between different
reference frames, as this seems to cause issues for students.


B.1.1 Converting between Cartesian coordinates and cylindrical polar coordi-
      nates




                                                        Figure B.2 Planar view of cylindrical polar coordi-
                                                        nates (or plane polar coordinates).




Figure B.1 Cylindrical polar coordinates.
We write a position vector in Cartesians as

                                               r = xi + yj + zk,                                         (B.1.1)

and as
                                                 r = rr̂ + zẑ,                                          (B.1.2)
in cylindrical polars. You need to know the following formulae for conversion from polar to Cartesian
coordinates:
                                 x = r cos θ,   y = r sin θ,  z = z,                          (B.1.3)

                                                      213
APPENDIX B. VECTOR CALCULUS                                                                                214

and these formulae for conversion back:
                                  p                                  y
                              r = x2 + y 2 ,           θ = arctan    ,    z = z.                      (B.1.4)
                                                                 x
Note that (B.1.4) can be derived by rearranging (B.1.3), and vice versa. You also need to know the following
definitions of the unit vectors:
                                                    
                                               cos θ
                                        r̂ =  sin θ  = cos θi + sin θj,                             (B.1.5)
                                                 0
                                                    
                                             − sin θ
                                     θ̂ =  cos θ  = − sin θi + cos θj,                              (B.1.6)
                                                0
                                                        ẑ = k̂,                                        (B.1.7)

and it might help to know that θ̂ = dr̂/dθ, which can be used to remember θ if you struggle. In addition,
you need to know
                         i = cos θr̂ − sin θθ̂,  j = sin θr̂ + cos θθ̂, k = ẑ,                   (B.1.8)
which can if necessary be derived by rearranging the formulae (B.1.5), (B.1.6) and (B.1.7). As you might
expect, these equations are all consistent with one another, so if you substitute (B.1.8) into (B.1.1) and use
(B.1.3) to rewrite x and y in terms of r and θ, you will end up with (B.1.2).
Scalar and vector quantities are converted as follows:
  • Conversion of scalar quantities: To convert a scalar quantity (e.g. lengths, pressures) from Cartesians
    to polars you substitute (B.1.3) and to go from polars to Cartesians use (B.1.4). For example if
                                                        p = x + 3y 2
     in Cartesians then, using (B.1.3),
                                                 p = r cos θ + 3r2 sin2 θ
     in polars.
  • Conversion of vector quantities: To convert a vector from cylindrical polar to Cartesian coordinates
    you will have a vector written something like this:
                                 u = ur (r, θ, z, t)r̂ + uθ (r, θ, z, t)θ̂ + uz (r, θ, z, t)ẑ,         (B.1.9)
     where ur , uθ and uz are all given scalar expressions. You need to end up with something of this form:
                                u = ux (x, y, z, t)i + uy (x, y, z, t)j + uz (x, y, z, t)k.           (B.1.10)
     Two methods:
        ◦ Substitute the expressions (B.1.5), (B.1.6) and (B.1.7) into (B.1.9), and then use (B.1.4) to get
          everything in terms of the Cartesian coordinates x and y. To convert the other way, substitute
          (B.1.8) into (B.1.10) and then use (B.1.3) to replace any instances of x or y by r and θ.
        ◦ Alternatively, take the dot product of (B.1.10) with r̂, θ̂ and ẑ, respectively, and simplify the
          resulting expressions using (B.1.5), (B.1.6) and (B.1.7), which gives
                                                     ur = u cos θ + v sin θ,                          (B.1.11)
                                                   uθ = −u sin θ + v cos θ,                           (B.1.12)
                                                              uz = w.                                 (B.1.13)
           Similarly, taking the dot product of (B.1.9) with i, j and k, respectively, and simplifying leads to
                                                   u = ur cos θ − uθ sin θ,                           (B.1.14)
                                                   v = ur sin θ + uθ cos θ,                           (B.1.15)
                                                              w = uz .                                (B.1.16)
APPENDIX B. VECTOR CALCULUS                                                                               215

What you need to know: You need to remember the formulae (B.1.3) and (B.1.4), but you would be given
(B.1.5), (B.1.6) and (B.1.7) or (B.1.8) in an exam question, but you need to be able to use them to do the
conversion yourself.

B.1.2 Converting between Cartesian coordinates and plane polar coordinates
Two-dimensional plane polars (r, θ) work like the first two coordinates of cylindrical polars. Thus converting
between plane polars and Cartesian coordinates works the same as for cylindrical coordinates and Cartesians
Subsection B.1.1, except there is no third dimension.
See for example Exercise 2.3.3 Part (b).
